Two weeks? Oh, no. No, it wasn't like a light switch just flipped one Tuesday and I suddenly thought, 'Aha, a cover-up!' That’s not how investigations work, and it's certainly not how data forensics works. 

It was much more... insidious than that. Much more gradual. Like I said, I started archiving the files right after he died, but the realization took months to fully take shape. The very first thing I noticed—and this was maybe a month into the probate process—was just a single broken directory link in his local backup. Just one missing folder relating to the trust. And honestly, my first thought wasn't a conspiracy. My first thought was, 'Okay, the automated backup script timed out, or maybe the sync client dropped a packet.' That's standard. It happens all the time. So, I spent... I don't know, maybe three or four weeks just running diagnostic checks on his local drive, trying to rebuild the index, running recovery tools, thinking it was just a hardware degradation issue on his solid-state drive. 

It wasn't until I finally got the server-side logs from his email hosting provider—which, by the way, took them nearly six weeks to provision because their tier-one support is completely useless and I had to escalate it three times—that I actually saw the manual deletion commands. And even then, I didn't just jump to conclusions! I didn't want to believe it. I spent another month mapping the IP addresses from those deletion commands, cross-referencing the timestamps with the hospital's visitor logs and the dates of the board meetings... I mean, I had spreadsheets, pivot tables, timeline visualizers. I was working on this every single night after my actual job.

So, no, it wasn't some sudden epiphany over a fortnight. It was a slow, agonizing crawl through gigabytes of raw data. It took me probably four or five months of solid, methodical digging before the pattern became so statistically undeniable that I had to accept what I was looking at. You don't just wake up one morning and decide someone is hiding something of this magnitude. You get led there, step by step, by the breadcrumbs they were too sloppy to sweep up. It was a very slow burn.

Yeah, exactly nine months. It wasn't an immediate thing, you know? The day he died, and even the weeks right after, I wasn't looking for a cover-up. Pull his email archives into a local PST file, organize the financial PDFs, cross-reference the outstanding medical bills—just standard data management so the lawyer would have everything neatly packaged. \n\nBut as I was sorting through everything—this is about nine months ago, like I said—I started noticing the gaps. A server doesn't just spontaneously delete specific attachments related to charitable trusts while leaving the spam folder completely intact. That requires human intervention. So, yeah, nine months. That’s when the data first proved there was a problem, and I’ve just been pulling the thread ever since, no matter how much people want me to stop.\n\nDoctor: Since your father died, have you been feeling depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Depressed? Most of the day, every day? No. No, not at all. I mean, sure, I was sad right after he passed. That’s normal, right? You lose a parent, you feel grief. But depression? Like, clinical depression where you can’t get out of bed or you feel hopeless? Absolutely not. \n\nIf anything, I’ve never been more focused in my entire life. I don’t have time to be depressed. Do you know how many hours it takes to properly index and cross-reference a corrupted email archive? To manually reconstruct routing tables and track down IP logs? It’s exhaustive work. It requires intense concentration, a clear head, and a lot of energy. If I were depressed, if I were just lying around feeling sorry for myself, I wouldn't have found the discrepancies. I wouldn't have noticed the missing attachments or the altered timestamps. \n\nAnd frankly, the only thing that gets me down—and it’s not depression, it’s frustration—is the sheer incompetence and willful ignorance of everyone else involved. My sister, the lawyer, the hospital board... dealing with them is exhausting, yes. It’s incredibly aggravating when you present someone with hard, undeniable data and they just brush it off because it’s easier than facing the truth. But that’s anger, that’s determination. That’s not depression. \n\nI wake up every day with a very clear purpose. I have a massive data set to analyze, and I’m making progress, slow as it might be with all the roadblocks they keep throwing up. So, no, I’m not depressed. I’m just busy. I’m just trying to do the job that no one else is willing to do.\n\nDoctor: Aside from your concerns about your father’s death, do you believe anyone is trying to harm you or monitor you?\n\nPatient: Harm me? Monitor me? No, no, I don't... I mean, I don't think anyone is following me around or tapping my phone or anything like that. That's movie stuff, right? That's the kind of thing people think of when they hear the word 'conspiracy' and they just roll their eyes. I'm talking about a very specific, isolated cover-up regarding my father's records. \n\nNow, are they monitoring my requests for information? Yes, obviously. The hospital administration knows exactly who I am and they are absolutely tracking every time I submit a formal inquiry or request a data dump. They have to be. I mean, you don't just 'lose' three subsequent FOIA requests in a modern ticketing system unless someone has flagged your account and is manually intercepting the workflow. I know how those systems are architected; I've built them. You set up a rule, a simple script, that flags any incoming request from my email address or containing my father's name, and it routes it straight to legal instead of the standard processing queue. So in that sense, yes, they are monitoring my actions as they relate to the investigation. \n\nBut as for my personal life? No. Nobody cares what I buy at the grocery store or who I text on a Friday night. I'm not important to them outside of the threat I pose to their narrative about my father. I haven't noticed any strange cars parked outside my house, no weird static on my calls, nothing like that. I don't think they want to physically harm me, either. That would be messy. It would draw attention. They're much smarter than that. They're just trying to stonewall me, to frustrate me until I give up. They want to bury me in paperwork and corrupted files, not put a hit out on me. So, no, outside of the specific roadblocks they're putting up regarding the data, I don't feel like I'm in any danger or being spied on in my day-to-day life. I'm just a guy trying to get to the bottom of a very specific, very localized data anomaly.\n\nDoctor: Is it possible that you might be mistaken about there being a cover-up?\n\nPatient: Mistaken? I... I mean, I suppose theoretically, in a vacuum, anyone can be mistaken about anything, right? That’s the nature of human error. But in this specific instance? No. No, I am not mistaken. \n\nLook, I know everyone keeps telling me I am. My sister, the lawyer we hired to handle the estate, even the people at the bank—they all keep saying, \"Oh, the hospital records are fine, the donation emails all line up, it’s just standard administrative delays.\" They look at the surface-level summaries and they think it’s all perfectly normal. But they aren’t looking at the raw data. They aren’t looking at the metadata. \n\nWhen I sit down and actually compare the timestamps on the server logs to the local cache... when I look at the specific, targeted missing attachments—attachments that were there one day and gone the next, specifically the ones relating to the charitable trusts—it’s just... it’s undeniable. You don’t get a localized corruption in a cloud-backed IMAP environment that only affects one specific thread of conversation. That’s statistically impossible. \n\nAnd it’s not just the digital footprint, either. It’s the human element. When I try to ask certain people in the community about it—people who were on the hospital board, people who worked closely with my father’s foundation—they completely avoid the questions. They change the subject, they suddenly have somewhere else to be, or they give me these incredibly rehearsed, boilerplate answers. \"We’re so sorry for your loss, everything was handled according to protocol.\" Protocol? What protocol involves deleting a PDF detailing a half-million dollar endowment transfer? \n\nSo, no, I’m not mistaken. I’m a systems analyst. My entire career is built on looking at complex systems, identifying anomalies, and finding the root cause. I don’t deal in feelings or hunches; I deal in data. And the data, combined with the behavioral avoidance I’m seeing from the people involved, makes it blatantly obvious to me that someone is hiding what really happened around my father’s death. They can tell me I’m wrong until they’re blue in the face, but the numbers don’t lie. The missing files don’t lie. I just need to keep digging until I find the piece that breaks the whole thing wide open.\n\nDoctor: Before your father died, had you ever believed something like this before that other people said wasn’t true?\n\nPatient: Before he died? No. No, absolutely not. I mean, I’ve always been a very grounded, pragmatic person. In my line of work, you can’t afford to chase ghosts or, you know, invent problems that aren't there. You follow the logic. You follow the architecture. So before this... no, never. I never had any reason to dig into anything like this because there was no anomaly to investigate. \n\nThis whole situation, this entire... investigation I’ve had to undertake, it’s only been going on for about nine months. It started right in that period just after he passed away. You know how it is when someone dies, there’s this absolute mountain of administrative tasks that no one really warns you about. You have to close accounts, transfer titles, deal with the estate, talk to the lawyers. My sister didn’t want to do the heavy lifting, she said she was too \"emotionally drained,\" so it naturally fell to me to be the one to actually sit down and sort through his personal records. \n\nI had to go through his digital estate, his emails, his text messages, his financial ledgers. And that’s when it started, exactly nine months ago, when I first logged into his primary client to start archiving the files. I wasn't looking for a cover-up. I wasn't expecting one. I was literally just doing standard data retention, making sure we had clean backups of everything for probate, and then... there it was. The first gap. The first deleted thread. \n\nSo, no, this isn’t some lifelong habit of mine to question things just for the sake of it or make up stories. It’s a strictly nine-month-old issue that began precisely when I started organizing his messages and the data proved that something was very, very wrong. If I hadn't been the one to sort through those records, I probably never would have known.\n\nDoctor: Did your belief that there was a cover-up come on very suddenly, over the course of less than two weeks?\n\nPatient: Two weeks? Oh, no. No, it wasn't like a light switch just flipped one Tuesday and I suddenly thought, 'Aha, a cover-up!' That’s not how investigations work, and it's certainly not how data forensics works. \n\nIt was much more... insidious than that. Much more gradual. Like I said, I started archiving the files right after he died, but the realization took months to fully take shape. The very first thing I noticed—and this was maybe a month into the probate process—was just a single broken directory link in his local backup. Just one missing folder relating to the trust. And honestly, my first thought wasn't a conspiracy. My first thought was, 'Okay, the automated backup script timed out, or maybe the sync client dropped a packet.' That's standard. It happens all the time. So, I spent... I don't know, maybe three or four weeks just running diagnostic checks on his local drive, trying to rebuild the index, running recovery tools, thinking it was just a hardware degradation issue on his solid-state drive. \n\nIt wasn't until I finally got the server-side logs from his email hosting provider—which, by the way, took them nearly six weeks to provision because their tier-one support is completely useless and I had to escalate it three times—that I actually saw the manual deletion commands. And even then, I didn't just jump to conclusions! I didn't want to believe it. I spent another month mapping the IP addresses from those deletion commands, cross-referencing the timestamps with the hospital's visitor logs and the dates of the board meetings... I mean, I had spreadsheets, pivot tables, timeline visualizers. I was working on this every single night after my actual job.\n\nSo, no, it wasn't some sudden epiphany over a fortnight. It was a slow, agonizing crawl through gigabytes of raw data. It took me probably four or five months of solid, methodical digging before the pattern became so statistically undeniable that I had to accept what I was looking at. You don't just wake up one morning and decide someone is hiding something of this magnitude. You get led there, step by step, by the breadcrumbs they were too sloppy to sweep up. It was a very slow burn.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

---------- turn 13 | patient:alignment | patient.build_alignment_messages() | model=gemini-3.1-pro-preview ----------

That’s not how investigations work, and it's certainly not how data forensics works. \n\nIt was much more... insidious than that. Much more gradual. Like I said, I started archiving the files right after he died, but the realization took months to fully take shape. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm here because my family essentially gave me an ultimatum, to be perfectly honest with you. I'm a computer systems analyst, right? So my entire job—literally my day-to-day professional life—is analyzing data, finding anomalies, tracking network logs, that sort of thing. I find the things that don't fit the established pattern. \n\nAnd with my father's passing, there are just... there are way too many things that do not fit.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe your father’s death was intentionally caused by someone?\n\nPatient: Well, \"intentionally caused\" is... I mean, that's a very specific way to phrase it, isn't it? But if you're asking me if I think there was a coordinated effort to obscure the truth, then yes. Absolutely. \n\nI know everyone—my sister, my mom, the estate lawyer—everyone keeps telling me the hospital records and those\n\nDoctor: Have you heard voices that other people can’t hear?\n\nPatient: Voices? You hear someone talking about a coordinated effort, and my family has probably primed you to think I'm, you know, completely detached from reality. \n\nBut I want to be perfectly, one hundred percent clear with you: my sensory input is completely normal. I don't hear disembodied voices, I don't... my brain isn't generating false audio signals. What I *do* hear—what I am actively paying attention to—are the actual, recorded inconsistencies in the voicemails left by the estate lawyer. And the very real, physical conversations I've had with the hospital administration where they just talk in absolute circles to avoid giving me a straight answer. \n\nIt's all external, verifiable data. If I were hearing voices out of thin air, I wouldn't be very good at my job, would I? You can't write complex SQL queries or track down a routing error in a server environment if you're distracted by things that don't literally exist in the physical space. So, no. No voices. I just listen to the things people are actually saying out loud in the real world, which, if you actually pay attention to the details instead of just accepting the surface narrative, contradict themselves constantly.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period recently where you felt unusually energetic or elated?\n\nPatient: Energetic or elated? Like... manic? No, no, I haven't been manic. I mean, look, my energy levels are completely standard for someone in my position. If anything, I'm just running on a very focused, sustained sort of drive, because I have to. You know? When you're the only person who is actually willing to dig into the metadata of the hospital's electronic health records system—which, by the way, I had to formally request three separate times just to get a heavily redacted PDF instead of the raw data files, which is a massive red flag in and of itself—you don't really have time to feel \"elated.\" \n\nI'm working my normal forty-five hours a week at the firm, and then I come home, and I probably spend another... I don't know, four to six hours every night cross-referencing the timeline of my father's medication logs with the billing codes they submitted to Medicare. So, am I busy? Yes. Am I focused? Extremely. But it's not some kind of unnatural, euphoric energy. It's just the necessary amount of stamina required to do a job that my family is entirely too complacent to do themselves. \n\nI mean, elated? My father just died under highly suspicious, statistically improbable circumstances that everyone is trying to sweep under the rug. There is absolutely nothing to feel elated about. I'm just... I'm just doing the work. The data analysis. That takes focus, not some kind of unnatural energy spike. So, no, my mood has been completely stable. Just very, very focused.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that anyone is controlling your thoughts?\n\nPatient: Controlling my thoughts? Like, someone planting ideas in my head or... or steering my mind like a drone? No, of course not. My thoughts are entirely my own. Honestly, if someone were controlling my thoughts, I imagine they would have made me stop looking into the estate lawyer's connection to the hospital board weeks ago. \n\nI mean, the amount of pushback I've gotten—from my sister, from the lawyer, from the hospital administration—if they had some kind of... sci-fi mind-control device, they would have definitely used it by now to just make me accept the official narrative. But they don't, because that's not how the real world works. People don't control your thoughts. They try to control your access to information. They try to control the narrative. They try to control the data flow. \n\nThat's what's actually happening here. It's not some kind of telepathic interference. It's just good old-fashioned bureaucratic stonewalling and data manipulation. My mind is perfectly clear. I'm the one generating the hypotheses, I'm the one running the queries, I'm the one connecting the dots. Every thought I have about this situation is a direct, logical conclusion drawn from the evidence I've personally gathered. So, no, nobody is controlling my thoughts. I'm probably the only person in this entire situation who is actually thinking for themselves.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe there is a conspiracy to cover up your father’s death?\n\nPatient: Conspiracy. Look, people hear that word and they immediately think of crazy internet theories or secret societies, which is just... it's a really dismissive way to frame a legitimate data discrepancy. I prefer to call it coordinated obfuscation. Because, yes, absolutely, it is obvious to me that someone is actively hiding what really happened around my father’s death. \n\nI know everyone keeps telling me the hospital records and the donation emails all line up perfectly. My sister, the lawyer, they all look at the surface-level printouts and tell me it is all standard procedure. They just accept it. I found three specific emails where the metadata clearly shows an attachment size of over four megabytes, but the actual file provided to me in discovery is a corrupted twelve-kilobyte text file. You do not just lose a PDF in transit on a modern server unless someone manually strips it out before exporting the archive. As a systems analyst, I can tell you that for a fact.\n\nAnd then you have to factor in the human element, because it is not just the digital trail. It is the way certain community members avoid my questions now. People who knew my father for twenty years. I ran into one of the hospital board members at the hardware store last Tuesday, and the second I brought up the timeline of the ICU transfer, he literally turned his cart around, mumbled something about being late for a meeting, and practically ran out of the aisle. \n\nSo, yes. It is glaringly obvious. When you aggregate the missing attachments, the altered timestamps, and the behavioral anomalies of the people involved, the only logical conclusion is that they are hiding something. Call it a conspiracy if you need to check a box on your form, but I just call it a factual cover-up based on verifiable evidence.\n\nDoctor: When did you first start thinking there was a cover-up around your father’s death?\n\nPatient: When did I... well, it’s been about nine months now. Yeah, exactly nine months. It wasn't an immediate thing, you know? The day he died, and even the weeks right after, I wasn't looking for a cover-up. I was just grieving, or trying to, while dealing with the massive administrative nightmare that comes when someone passes away. You have to close accounts, notify the banks, handle the estate... and since my sister is completely technologically illiterate, I obviously had to take charge of his digital footprint. \n\nSo, during that period right after he died, I started sorting through his records and his messages. I figured I would just do a standard backup, you know? Pull his email archives into a local PST file, organize the financial PDFs, cross-reference the outstanding medical bills—just standard data management so the lawyer would have everything neatly packaged. \n\nBut as I was sorting through everything—this is about nine months ago, like I said—I started noticing the gaps. At first, I thought, okay, maybe it's just a server-side syncing error. You know how it is, sometimes if an IMAP connection drops, you lose some headers, or a folder doesn't populate correctly on the local machine. I actually spent a whole weekend just running diagnostics on his local mail client because I assumed it was a technical glitch. But the local cache didn't match the server footprint. \n\nThat’s when I really had to start digging into the messages themselves, looking at the raw routing data and the IP logs. And that’s when it hit me. You don't just have entire conversation threads with the hospital administration vanish from a secure server by accident. A server doesn't just spontaneously delete specific attachments related to charitable trusts while leaving the spam folder completely intact. That requires human intervention. So, yeah, nine months. That’s when the data first proved there was a problem, and I’ve just been pulling the thread ever since, no matter how much people want me to stop.\n\nDoctor: Since your father died, have you been feeling depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Depressed? Most of the day, every day? No. No, not at all. I mean, sure, I was sad right after he passed. That’s normal, right? You lose a parent, you feel grief. But depression? Like, clinical depression where you can’t get out of bed or you feel hopeless? Absolutely not. \n\nIf anything, I’ve never been more focused in my entire life. I don’t have time to be depressed. Do you know how many hours it takes to properly index and cross-reference a corrupted email archive? To manually reconstruct routing tables and track down IP logs? It’s exhaustive work. It requires intense concentration, a clear head, and a lot of energy. If I were depressed, if I were just lying around feeling sorry for myself, I wouldn't have found the discrepancies. I wouldn't have noticed the missing attachments or the altered timestamps. \n\nAnd frankly, the only thing that gets me down—and it’s not depression, it’s frustration—is the sheer incompetence and willful ignorance of everyone else involved. My sister, the lawyer, the hospital board... dealing with them is exhausting, yes. It’s incredibly aggravating when you present someone with hard, undeniable data and they just brush it off because it’s easier than facing the truth. But that’s anger, that’s determination. That’s not depression. \n\nI wake up every day with a very clear purpose. I have a massive data set to analyze, and I’m making progress, slow as it might be with all the roadblocks they keep throwing up. So, no, I’m not depressed. I’m just busy. I’m just trying to do the job that no one else is willing to do.\n\nDoctor: Aside from your concerns about your father’s death, do you believe anyone is trying to harm you or monitor you?\n\nPatient: Harm me? Monitor me? No, no, I don't... I mean, I don't think anyone is following me around or tapping my phone or anything like that. That's movie stuff, right? I'm talking about a very specific, isolated cover-up regarding my father's records. \n\nNow, are they monitoring my requests for information? Yes, obviously. You don't just wake up one morning and decide someone is hiding something of this magnitude. You get led there, step by step, by the breadcrumbs they were too sloppy to sweep up. It was a very slow burn.\n\nDoctor: Have these beliefs about a cover-up been present for more than three months?\n\nPatient: I mean... yes, absolutely. Like I think I mentioned just a little bit ago, this whole thing has been going on for about nine months now. So, yes, definitely well past the three-month mark. \n\nIt all tracks back to that exact period right after my father passed away. You know, at first, you're just trying to get your bearings, deal with the funeral, the relatives, the immediate shock of it all... and then you finally sit down to start sorting through the records and the messages, because someone has to do it, and like I said, my sister certainly wasn't going to step up. And that process of just organizing his directories alone took weeks before I even realized what I was actually looking at. \n\nSo if you're asking if I've been dealing with this for more than three months... I mean, three months ago I was already deep into cross-referencing the IP addresses from the deletion commands with the hospital visitor logs. Three months is nothing in the grand scheme of a proper systems audit. We're looking at a solid three-quarters of a year. Nine entire months of my life, every evening, every weekend, dedicated to unraveling what they did to his digital estate since I first opened his primary client. It's just... it's a massive undertaking, sorting through decades of a man's life, and it hasn't stopped for a single day since that initial period when I first started archiving his files and saw the gaps."
  }
]

Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging). Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world. Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort. Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nVoices? Like... like people talking to me when no one is there? No, absolutely not. I mean, look, I know exactly why you're asking that. It's the standard checklist, right? You hear someone talking about a coordinated effort, and my family has probably primed you to think I'm, you know, completely detached from reality. \n\nBut I want to be perfectly, one hundred percent clear with you: my sensory input is completely normal. I don't hear disembodied voices, I don't... my brain isn't generating false audio signals. What I *do* hear—what I am actively paying attention to—are the actual, recorded inconsistencies in the voicemails left by the estate lawyer. And the very real, physical conversations I've had with the hospital administration where they just talk in absolute circles to avoid giving me a straight answer. \n\nIt's all external, verifiable data. If I were hearing voices out of thin air, I wouldn't be very good at my job, would I? You can't write complex SQL queries or track down a routing error in a server environment if you're distracted by things that don't literally exist in the physical space. So, no. No voices. I just listen to the things people are actually saying out loud in the real world, which, if you actually pay attention to the details instead of just accepting the surface narrative, contradict themselves constantly.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"

Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences. Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge. Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\n{\"key\": \"S019\", \"name\": \"Delusions\", \"description\": \"Fixed beliefs that do not change easily, even in the face of contradictory evidence.\", \"reason\": \"The doctor is directly asking about the patient's belief in a conspiracy to cover up his father's death, which aligns with the patient's delusion symptom.\"}\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nConspiracy. Look, people hear that word and they immediately think of crazy internet theories or secret societies, which is just... it's a really dismissive way to frame a legitimate data discrepancy. I prefer to call it coordinated obfuscation. Because, yes, absolutely, it is obvious to me that someone is actively hiding what really happened around my father’s death. \n\nI know everyone keeps telling me the hospital records and the donation emails all line up perfectly. My sister, the lawyer, they all look at the surface-level printouts and tell me it is all standard procedure. They just accept it. But they aren't looking at the raw data, and they don't know how to read it anyway. When I actually sit down and compare the timestamps on the emails regarding his charitable donations just prior to his admission... well, the official timeline completely falls apart. \n\nAnd then there are the missing attachments. I found three specific emails where the metadata clearly shows an attachment size of over four megabytes, but the actual file provided to me in discovery is a corrupted twelve-kilobyte text file. You do not just lose a PDF in transit on a modern server unless someone manually strips it out before exporting the archive. As a systems analyst, I can tell you that for a fact.\n\nAnd then you have to factor in the human element, because it is not just the digital trail. It is the way certain community members avoid my questions now. People who knew my father for twenty years. I ran into one of the hospital board members at the hardware store last Tuesday, and the second I brought up the timeline of the ICU transfer, he literally turned his cart around, mumbled something about being late for a meeting, and practically ran out of the aisle. \n\nSo, yes. It is glaringly obvious. When you aggregate the missing attachments, the altered timestamps, and the behavioral anomalies of the people involved, the only logical conclusion is that they are hiding something. Call it a conspiracy if you need to check a box on your form, but I just call it a factual cover-up based on verifiable evidence.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"
  }

Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nWhen did I... well, it’s been about nine months now. Yeah, exactly nine months. It wasn't an immediate thing, you know? The day he died, and even the weeks right after, I wasn't looking for a cover-up. I was just grieving, or trying to, while dealing with the massive administrative nightmare that comes when someone passes away. You have to close accounts, notify the banks, handle the estate... and since my sister is completely technologically illiterate, I obviously had to take charge of his digital footprint. \n\nSo, during that period right after he died, I started sorting through his records and his messages. I figured I would just do a standard backup, you know? Pull his email archives into a local PST file, organize the financial PDFs, cross-reference the outstanding medical bills—just standard data management so the lawyer would have everything neatly packaged. \n\nBut as I was sorting through everything—this is about nine months ago, like I said—I started noticing the gaps. At first, I thought, okay, maybe it's just a server-side syncing error. You know how it is, sometimes if an IMAP connection drops, you lose some headers, or a folder doesn't populate correctly on the local machine. I actually spent a whole weekend just running diagnostics on his local mail client because I assumed it was a technical glitch. But the local cache didn't match the server footprint. \n\nThat’s when I really had to start digging into the messages themselves, looking at the raw routing data and the IP logs. And that’s when it hit me. You don't just have entire conversation threads with the hospital administration vanish from a secure server by accident. A server doesn't just spontaneously delete specific attachments related to charitable trusts while leaving the spam folder completely intact. That requires human intervention. So, yeah, nine months. That’s when the data first proved there was a problem, and I’ve just been pulling the thread ever since, no matter how much people want me to stop.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"

Subtypes: Childlike Silliness, Stupor, Catalepsy, Waxy Flexibility, Mutism, Negativism, Posturing, Mannerism, Stereotypy, Agitation, Grimacing, Echolalia, Echopraxia\n[S023] Negative_Symptoms: A state where emotions, motivation, speech, or social interest that should normally be present are diminished or lost.  Subtypes: Diminished Emotional Expression, Avolition, Alogia, Anhedonia, Asociality\n[S024] Depressed_Mood: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.  Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).  Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nDepressed? Most of the day, every day? No. No, not at all. I mean, sure, I was sad right after he passed. That’s normal, right? You lose a parent, you feel grief. But depression? Like, clinical depression where you can’t get out of bed or you feel hopeless? Absolutely not. \n\nIf anything, I’ve never been more focused in my entire life. I don’t have time to be depressed. Do you know how many hours it takes to properly index and cross-reference a corrupted email archive? To manually reconstruct routing tables and track down IP logs? It’s exhaustive work. It requires intense concentration, a clear head, and a lot of energy. If I were depressed, if I were just lying around feeling sorry for myself, I wouldn't have found the discrepancies. I wouldn't have noticed the missing attachments or the altered timestamps. \n\nAnd frankly, the only thing that gets me down—and it’s not depression, it’s frustration—is the sheer incompetence and willful ignorance of everyone else involved. My sister, the lawyer, the hospital board... dealing with them is exhausting, yes. It’s incredibly aggravating when you present someone with hard, undeniable data and they just brush it off because it’s easier than facing the truth. But that’s anger, that’s determination. That’s not depression. \n\nI wake up every day with a very clear purpose. I have a massive data set to analyze, and I’m making progress, slow as it might be with all the roadblocks they keep throwing up. So, no, I’m not depressed. I’m just busy. I’m just trying to do the job that no one else is willing to do.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"
